        const roll: u64 = stone_sounds.random.next();
        const took_stones: bool = report.captured_count > 0;
        if (took_stones) {
            const take: u8 = nextTake(stone_sounds.last_capture, roll, capture_oggs.len);
            stone_sounds.last_capture = take;
            z.sounds.play(&stone_sounds.audio.sounds, stone_sounds.captures[take]);
            return;
        }
        const take: u8 = nextTake(stone_sounds.last_click, roll, move_oggs.len);
        stone_sounds.last_click = take;
        z.sounds.play(&stone_sounds.audio.sounds, stone_sounds.clicks[take]);
    }
};

11.7 The program

The three pictures are part of the program, embedded at compile time; at start they are decoded, uploaded to the GPU, and their decoded pixels freed.

uploadPng
/// Decodes a PNG, uploads it as a texture, and frees the decoded pixels, which the GPU now holds.
fn uploadPng(gpa: Allocator, gl: *z.WgpuGl, png_bytes: []const u8) !z.WgpuTexture {
    const image: z.Image = try z.loadImageFromMemory(gpa, png_bytes);
    const texture: z.WgpuTexture = z.loadTextureFromImage(gl, image);
    z.unloadImage(gpa, image);
    return texture;
}
init
/// Writes the whole state: the pictures and font loaded, the interface made, the main menu shown - and a game started
/// with its choices behind it, so the state always holds a game.
fn init(gpa: Allocator, f: *z.Frame, s: *State, variant: AppVariant) !void {
    const font: z.Font = try z.loadFont(f, gpa, atkinson_mono_ttf, 20);
    // The audio device opens with the app: on a page, that is what makes the page ask for a press before it sounds.
    z.audio_device.init(f.audio_device);
    const controls: ControlsStyle = .{};
    const menu: MenuChoices = .{};
    const computer_settings: ComputerSettings = .{};
    s.* = .{
        .variant = variant,
        .won_at_seconds = null,
        .ladder_position = 0,
        .problem_index = 0,
        .problem_tree = .empty,
        .problem_solved_once = false,
        .problem_refuted = false,
        .problem_kind = .kill,
        .computer_hopeless_moves = 0,
        .hero = .{},
        .random = Xoshiro256.init(@trunc(z.epochMillis())),
        .gpa = gpa,
        .problem_play = .{},
        .screen = .main_menu,
        .menu = menu,
        .game = undefined,
        .new_game_settings = menu.settings(),
        .players = menu.players(),
        .computer_settings = computer_settings,
        .computer = try ComputerPlayer.init(gpa, computer_settings),
        .style = .{},
        .controls = controls,
        .hold = .empty,
        .ghost = .hidden,
        .overlays = .{},
        .hover_clock = .{},
        .flights = .none,
        .slot_spacing_spring = .atRest(0),
        .slot_spacing_ready = false,
        .ghost_position = undefined,
        .pause_open = false,
        .window_heights = .{},
        .finger_gesture = .uncancelled,
        .stone_sounds = try StoneSounds.load(gpa, f, controls.sound_volume, variant.reward_sound),
        .sound_on = true,
        .debug = .{},
        .move_log = undefined,
        .ui_host = z.UiHost.init(gpa, font),
        .textures = .{
            .wood = try uploadPng(gpa, f.gl, wood_png),
            .black_stone = try uploadPng(gpa, f.gl, black_stone_png),
            .white_stone = try uploadPng(gpa, f.gl, white_stone_png),
        },
        .font = font,
    };
    startGame(&s.game, s.new_game_settings);
    // Go puzzles hero starts its first round, a fight; its fixed game is that fight's, not one to start here.
    if (variant.hero) {
        const progress: HeroProgress = loadHeroProgress(variant);
        s.hero.wins = progress.wins;
        s.hero.rating = progress.rating;
        s.ladder_position = progress.ladder_position;
        s.hero.unlocked = progress.unlocked;
        beginHeroRound(s);
        return;
    }
    // A variant with problems starts the first: the person black against the computer.
    if (variant.problems.len > 0) {
        s.players = .{ .kind = .against_computer, .person_stone = .black };
        beginProblem(s);
    }
    // A variant with a game of its own starts it at once: there is no main menu to choose one in.
    if (variant.fixed_game) |fixed_game| {
        s.players = fixed_game.players;
        s.screen = .game;
        var first_settings: NewGameSettings = fixed_game.settings;
        if (variant.outpost_ladder) |ladder| {
            first_settings = ladder.settingsAt(first_settings, s.ladder_position, s.players.person_stone);
        }
        beginGame(s, first_settings);
    }
}
app
/// The program, as the runner (a page, a window, a headless run) or the launcher starts it: goban, with its interface.
pub const app: z.AppSpec(State) = appFor(.{});

The same file is also built as its own test, with nothing of the engine: the tests reach only the rules and the input, so only those are compiled. The one declaration the compiler always looks at, the panic handler, chooses between the engine's and the standard library's at compile time, so the test build never needs the engine.

panic
/// The panic handler: the examples' own, which reports through the engine, when this file is the program; std's
/// default when this file is built as its own test, which has no engine to report through.
pub const panic = if (builtin.is_test) std.debug.FullPanic(std.debug.defaultPanic) else std.debug.FullPanic(
    common.reportPanic,
);

/// One of goban's apps, as `variant` makes it.
pub fn appFor(comptime variant: AppVariant) z.AppSpec(State) {
    const VariantInit = struct {
        fn initVariant(gpa: Allocator, f: *z.Frame, s: *State) !void {
            try init(gpa, f, s, variant);
        }
    };
    const spec: z.AppSpec(State) = .{
        .config = .{
            .window = .{
                .title = variant.title,
                .width = 900,
                .height = 900,
                .scale_mode = .responsive,
            },
        },
        .init = VariantInit.initVariant,
        .memory = .managed,
        .deinit = deinit,
        .update = update,
    };
    return spec;
}

11.8 Another app from the same code

goban's app is made by one function, appFor, from a description of what makes it: a fixed game or the main menu's choice, an interface or none, and whether a won game starts again by itself. goban itself is appFor(.{}), everything at its default. Another example makes its own app from the same code by importing goban's file as a module and calling appFor with a description of its own, so the rules, the computer, the board, the input and the sounds are goban's, not a copy.

goban outposts is such an app: Atari Go on 8 x 8 against the computer, the person black, two outposts to begin with, on a ladder of games (5.5), no menu, no button and no caption - the screen is the board, with the ladder's level in its corner - and a won game starting again a moment after its capture, the next game the one the result leads to on the ladder, its outposts scattered anew. goban problems is another: its kill problems (5.6) against the computer, one after another, with a kill problem's interface - Undo, and Next once the target is captured. go puzzles hero is a third: rounds of three kinds - a fight, Atari Go on 10 x 10 with outposts on their ladder, which keeps the fights balanced - the outposts on the second and third lines, but never on the third line level with the starting square, where one would start a ladder against its stones at once; a life puzzle; a kill puzzle - each puzzle a ladder (5.7), and each puzzle drawn from the player's rating (5.8). The next round is drawn at random, its chances set by the round before: after a fight, a kill puzzle twice as often as a life puzzle; after a life puzzle, a kill puzzle twice as often as a fight; after a kill puzzle, any of the three alike. Only a kill puzzle follows one of its own kind, and in the long run a quarter of the rounds are fights, a quarter life puzzles and half kill puzzles. There is no undo, and no giving up: a fight is played to its end, and a puzzle to its result. Losing a puzzle gives its next, later start, down to its last; solving a step goes back one, towards the full problem, and solving that ends the round; a step failed five times in a row moves on to the next round, so that no puzzle holds the player for good. Once the round's problem has been failed three times, on any of its steps, a Hint button shows in the bottom-left corner: it shows the move to play as a blue dot for half a second - the book's next correct move while the moves so far follow the book, or else the search's choice for the player. Its screen says little. In the top-left corner, the player's level: it starts at 1 and rises by one every ten wins - a round's full problem solved at its first try, or a fight won - kept between runs. Along the bottom, between the buttons, a row of ten rings fills with black stones, one for each win since the level began. When the last ring fills, the game waits half a second, the full row in view, then a picture drawn at random from a set of photographs - humorous cats - comes down from above the window to its centre in half a second, slowing into place, stays two seconds, and goes on down and out in half a second, gathering speed, a backdrop darkening and clearing with it, to cheering and clapping; then the row empties and the level rises. A picture is decoded when it is shown and freed after, so the set costs memory only in the page's size. Across the top, the round's kind - Fight, Live or Kill - until the player's first move, then Solved or Failed once a puzzle is: Failed when a kill puzzle's group is alive beyond doubt or a life puzzle's group is captured. The group to save or to kill is marked only when another group of its colour could be meant, and only until the first move. The description is all its program says; its jobs are goban's too, its kernel file naming goban's.

Interface
/// What interface one of goban's apps has.
pub const Interface = enum {
    /// The buttons, the windows, the debug panel and the caption.
    full,
    /// None: the screen is the board alone.
    none,
    /// A problem's: Undo, and Next once the problem is solved.
    problem_buttons,
    /// Go puzzles hero's: the hint once a puzzle has been failed often enough, Next once a puzzle is solved or lost,
    /// the count of wins in a corner, and a line across the top - the puzzle's kind before the first move, "Solved"
    /// once it is.
    hero_buttons,
};
AppVariant
/// What makes one of goban's apps (the section's lesson): goban is the default, and another example makes its own
/// from the same code with `appFor` - the rules, the search, the board, the input and the sounds all goban's.
pub const AppVariant = struct {
    /// The window's title.
    title: []const u8 = "zimr - goban",
    /// A game played from the start, with no main menu; null: the main menu chooses one.
    fixed_game: ?FixedGame = null,
    /// What interface the screen has.
    interface: Interface = .full,
    /// Problems played one after another, each an SGF text with its target marked (`problem_sgfs`); empty,
    /// the fixed game or the main menu's.
    problems: []const []const u8 = &.{},
    /// Go puzzles hero (`HeroState`): rounds of the fixed game (the fight, on its outpost ladder) and of puzzles from
    /// `problem_families`, in turn - fight, live, kill - each puzzle a ladder of starts, failed down and solved up.
    hero: bool = false,
    problem_families: []const ProblemFamily = &.{},
    /// Where go puzzles hero keeps its progress between runs (`HeroProgress`, `z.saveValue`: the page's local storage,
    /// a file natively).
    hero_progress_key: []const u8 = "go_puzzles_hero.progress",
    /// Where an earlier version kept the count of wins alone, read when there is no progress yet.
    hero_earlier_wins_key: []const u8 = "go_puzzles_hero.wins",
    /// The pictures go puzzles hero shows, one at random, at each new level (`RewardSettings`), the sound that goes
    /// with them (Ogg Vorbis), and the reward's timing; no pictures, no reward.
    reward_images: []const []const u8 = &.{},
    reward_sound: ?[]const u8 = null,
    reward: RewardSettings = .{},
    /// The window that asks a player for support after a session's first puzzles (`Paywall`), or none.
    paywall: ?Paywall = null,
    /// Whether a problem's target is marked on the board - a triangle on a stone to capture, a square on one to save.
    /// Where the problem makes its group plain enough, the board goes without.
    marks_target: bool = true,
    /// With kill problems, whether the computer answers with the book's reply when the moves so far are a line of the
    /// problem's solution tree - so that a player who finds the book's solution reaches its "Correct" - and searches
    /// only off the book.
    computer_follows_book: bool = true,
    /// A won game starts again by itself this long after the win, in seconds; null: the victory window offers what
    /// comes next.
    restart_after_win_seconds: ?f32 = null,
    /// Scattered stones that move with the results, against the computer: null, the fixed game's own.
    outpost_ladder: ?OutpostLadder = null,
};

OutpostLadder
/// A ladder of games that follows the results (`AppVariant.outpost_ladder`; the section's lesson): each rung a split of
/// scattered stones between the person and the computer, and each rung played twice - the person moving first, then
/// moving second. Its positions, from the easiest:
///
///     rung   person's  computer's   moving first, then second
///     0      2         0            positions 0, 1
///     1      2         1            2, 3
///     2      2         2            4, 5
///     3      1         2            6, 7
///     4      1         3            8, 9
///     5      1         4            10, 11
///     6      0         4            12, 13
///     7      0         5            14, 15
///     ...    0         one more each rung, as long as they fit the board
///
/// A win moves the person one position up, a loss one down, within the ladder: so a rung is climbed only by winning
/// it moving second, without the first move's advantage, and a loss moving second first gives the move back. The
/// rungs go up in threes: the computer given a stone, then another, then one of the person's taken - until the person
/// has none; from there each rung gives the computer one more, up to the most the board holds. The level the person
/// is at is the position counted from 1: level 1 at the bottom, which a loss does not go under.
pub const OutpostLadder = struct {
    /// The person's stones on the first rung.
    person_start: u8,

    /// The rung of `position`, two positions to a rung.
    fn rungOf(position: u8) u8 {
        return position / 2;
    }

    /// The person's and the computer's stones on `rung`, the rungs in threes: in the third `cycle`, the person has
    /// `person_start - cycle` and the computer `2 * cycle`, and the cycle's three rungs give the computer none, one and
    /// two more. Past the person's last stone, each rung gives the computer one more.
    fn rungSplit(ladder: OutpostLadder, rung: u8) [2]u8 {
        const rungs_per_cycle: u8 = 3;
        const cycle_rungs: u8 = rungs_per_cycle * ladder.person_start;
        if (rung <= cycle_rungs) {
            const cycle: u8 = rung / rungs_per_cycle;
            const step_in_cycle: u8 = rung % rungs_per_cycle;
            const person_count: u8 = ladder.person_start - cycle;
            const computer_count: u8 = 2 * cycle + step_in_cycle;
            return .{ person_count, computer_count };
        }
        const computer_count: u8 = 2 * ladder.person_start + (rung - cycle_rungs);
        return .{ 0, computer_count };
    }

    /// How many positions the ladder has on a board this size: two for each rung whose stones fit it.
    pub fn positionCount(ladder: OutpostLadder, board_size: u8) u8 {
        var top_rung: u8 = 0;
        while (true) {
            const split: [2]u8 = ladder.rungSplit(top_rung + 1);
            const next_rung_stones: ScatteredStones = .{ .black_count = split[0], .white_count = split[1] };
            if (!scatteredStonesFit(board_size, next_rung_stones)) {
                break;
            }
            top_rung += 1;
        }
        return 2 * (top_rung + 1);
    }

    /// The scattered stones of the game at `position`, the person playing `person_stone`: the rung's split.
    pub fn stonesAt(
        ladder: OutpostLadder,
        position: u8,
        person_stone: Stone,
    ) ScatteredStones {
        const split: [2]u8 = ladder.rungSplit(rungOf(position));
        const person_count: u8 = split[0];
        const computer_count: u8 = split[1];
        var stones: ScatteredStones = .{};
        if (person_stone == .black) {
            stones.black_count = person_count;
            stones.white_count = computer_count;
        } else {
            stones.black_count = computer_count;
            stones.white_count = person_count;
        }
        return stones;
    }

    /// Who moves first in the game at `position`: the person on a rung's first game, the computer on its second.
    pub fn firstPlayerAt(
        ladder: OutpostLadder,
        position: u8,
        person_stone: Stone,
    ) Stone {
        _ = ladder;
        const person_moves_first: bool = position % 2 == 0;
        return if (person_moves_first) person_stone else person_stone.opponent();
    }

    /// The position after a game at `position` on a board this size that `winner` won: one up after the person's win,
    /// one down after the computer's, within the ladder; the same after a game that ended undecided.
    pub fn nextPosition(
        ladder: OutpostLadder,
        board_size: u8,
        position: u8,
        winner: Stone,
        person_stone: Stone,
    ) u8 {
        if (winner == .none) {
            return position;
        }
        const person_won: bool = winner == person_stone;
        if (person_won) {
            return @min(position + 1, ladder.positionCount(board_size) - 1);
        }
        return position -| 1;
    }

    /// The settings of the game at `position`: `base` with the rung's stones and its first player.
    pub fn settingsAt(
        ladder: OutpostLadder,
        base: NewGameSettings,
        position: u8,
        person_stone: Stone,
    ) NewGameSettings {
        var settings: NewGameSettings = base;
        settings.scattered = ladder.stonesAt(position, person_stone);
        settings.first_player = ladder.firstPlayerAt(position, person_stone);
        return settings;
    }
};
submitInterface
/// One frame of the game screen, in the order the section's lesson gives; the interface is begun and rendered by
/// `update`.
/// Submits the game screen's interface (the section's lesson): the board's two buttons, disabled while a window is
/// open (G11), the open window, and the debug panel - writing what they ask for into `presses`, `pause_choice`,
/// `victory_choice` and `debug_requests`, for the game screen to carry out.
fn submitInterface(
    f: *z.Frame,
    s: *State,
    u: ui.Ui,
    window_width_px: f32,
    window_height_px: f32,
    font_size_px: f32,
    window_is_open: bool,
    presses: *ButtonPresses,
    pause_choice: *PauseChoice,
    victory_choice: *VictoryChoice,
    debug_requests: *DebugRequests,
) void {
    u.beginDisabled(window_is_open);
    const undo_takes_something_back: bool = movesUndoTakesBack(s.players, &s.game.record) > 0;
    presses.* = submitGameButtons(
        u,
        window_width_px,
        window_height_px,
        font_size_px,
        s.controls,
        undo_takes_something_back,
    );
    u.endDisabled();
    if (s.pause_open) {
        pause_choice.* = submitPauseWindow(
            u,
            window_width_px,
            window_height_px,
            font_size_px,
            s.controls,
            &s.window_heights,
            &s.overlays,
            &s.sound_on,
            &s.debug.open,
        );
    }
    if (s.game.position.winner) |winner| {
        victory_choice.* = submitVictoryWindow(
            u,
            winner,
            s.players.kind == .two_players,
            window_width_px,
            window_height_px,
            font_size_px,
            s.controls,
            &s.window_heights,
        );
    }
    // H opens and closes the debug panel; the pause window has a switch for it too.
    if (z.isKeyPressed(f.input, .h)) {
        s.debug.open = !s.debug.open;
    }
    if (s.debug.open) {
        const log_shown: []const MoveReport = s.move_log[0..s.game.record.moves_played];
        debug_requests.* = submitDebugPanel(
            u,
            &s.game,
            &s.computer,
            s.computer_settings,
            log_shown,
            s.debug.inspected_point,
            &s.debug,
            font_size_px,
        );
    }
}
goban outposts' variant
/// What makes this app of goban's: one game, played again and again, and nothing on the screen but the board.
const variant: goban.AppVariant = .{
    .title = "zimr - goban outposts",
    .fixed_game = .{
        .settings = .{ .board_size = 8, .capture_goal = 1, .scattered = .{ .black_count = 2 } },
        .players = .{ .kind = .against_computer, .person_stone = .black },
    },
    .interface = .none,
    // Long enough to see the capture: the captured stone's flight to its column, and its sound.
    .restart_after_win_seconds = 1.5,
    .outpost_ladder = .{ .person_start = 2 },
};

12. Reference

Every public declaration of goban.zig, with the first sentence of its documentation.

examples/goban/goban.zig

declarationwhat it is
board_limitsThe sizes a board may have.
PointA point of the board, as its index in reading order: row * board_size + col.
PointSetA set of points: one bit per point of the largest board.
StoneWhat stands on a point: nothing, a black stone or a white stone.
pointCountHow many points a board of this size has: board_size * board_size.
isOnBoardIs (row, col) a point of the board? Rows and columns are signed here, because the questions this answers come from arithmetic that can step off the board on either side: a neighbour's row - 1, or a pointer left of the board.
pointAtThe point at (row, col): its index in reading order, row * board_size + col.
rowOfThe row of a point: how many whole rows come before it in reading order.
colOfThe column of a point: how far along its row it is.
neighborsOfThe neighbours of point: the points one line away, up, down, left and right, in that order, leaving out the ones that would be off the board.
ProblemRegionA rectangle of the board's points, its first and last row and column included: the region a problem is played in (section 3's lesson).
PositionWhat is on the board now, and everything the rules need to know about it.
isRootDoes point hold a stone that is its own parent - the root of its group?
findRootThe root of the group that the stone on point belongs to: follow parents until a stone is its own parent.
findRootCompressingPathThe root of point's group, as findRoot finds it, and on the way every stone of the path is made a child of the root ("path compression"), so the next search from any of them takes one step.
joinGroupsJoins two groups of one colour into one, rooted at new_root: the joining group's root takes new_root as its parent, so its whole tree hangs under new_root, and its liberties and stones move into new_root's sets.
removeCapturedGroupTakes a captured group off the board and returns the points it emptied.
LegalityWhy a move may not be played - or that it may.
MoveReportWhat a move did: for the move log, the prisoners' flights and the tests.
wouldBeSuicideWould a stone of the player to move, placed on the empty point, be left with no liberty? It survives if any neighbour gives it a way out: - an empty neighbour is a liberty of its own; - a friendly group with 2 or more liberties still has one after this stone fills one, and the stone joins it; - an enemy group with exactly 1 liberty - this point - is captured, and its stones' points become liberties.
checkMoveMay the player to move place a stone on point, and if not, why not? The one definition of a legal move, asked by everything that needs to know, so the answer cannot differ from one place to another.
placeStonePlays a legal move: a stone of the player to move on point, then everything the rules make follow from it, in the six steps of the section's lesson.
prisonersOfHow many stones capturer has captured: its prisoners.
groupsCapturedByThe groups a move on point would capture, as their roots, written into the caller's buffer: the enemy groups beside the point whose one liberty is the point.
groupsTouchingThe groups touching point - of either colour - as their roots, written into the caller's buffer, each listed once however many sides of the point it touches.
positionAfterMoveThe position a legal move on point would make, written into after; position is left as it is.
aliveStonesThe stones of colour that are alive unconditionally (the section's lesson: Benson's algorithm) - alive whatever the opponent plays, though their owner never move again.
isUnconditionallyAliveWhether the chain at point is alive unconditionally (aliveStones).
record_limitsThe record's capacity.
atari_goThe rules of Atari Go that the board size depends on.
SetupStoneA stone that stands on the board before the first move: part of the record, not a move.
ScatteredStonesStones scattered on the board before the first move, around Atari Go's starting square (the section's lesson): so many black and so many white, each drawn at random from the lines nearest_line to farthest_line from the edge - the second by default, never the edge itself - at least min_distance_from_square from the square.
NewGameSettingsWhat a new game is played with.
RecordHow the game got to its position: replaying it from an empty board rebuilds the position.
GameA game: the position on the board, and the record it was replayed from.
putSetupStonePuts a setup stone on the board: placed by placeStone, so the forest grows as it would for a move, then stripped of what only a move leaves - the last-move marker and the ko point.
replayToRebuilds the position from the record: an empty board, the setup, then the first moves_played moves.
allowsBoardSizeMay a game with this capture goal (null for plain Go) be played on a board of this size? Plain Go needs board_limits.min_size points per side and Atari Go atari_go.min_board_size; neither may exceed board_limits.max_size.
startingSquareCornerThe top-left stone's row and column of Atari Go's starting square on a board this size: the square is that point, the one right of it, and the two below.
distanceFromStartingSquareHow far point is from Atari Go's starting square: the larger of its row and column steps to the square's nearest stone - so the points at one distance make a square ring around it.
linesFromEdgeHow many lines point is in from the board's nearest edge: 0 on the edge, 1 on the second line.
scatteredStonesFitWhether scattered's stones always fit a board this size: the first stone needs one candidate point, and each stone after it takes its own and those of its neighbours that are candidates, which no later stone may take - on the second line, at most two, the points beside it along the line.
smallestBoardForScatteredStonesThe smallest board on which scattered's stones always fit, or null when none does.
startGameStarts a new game: an empty record with the settings' size and goal, the Atari Go square as its setup when there is a capture goal - and the settings' scattered stones around it - and the position replayed from it.
recordIsFullHas the record no room for one more move after the ones the position shows? Moves undone do not count: a new move replaces them.
playMovePlays a legal move and records it.
undoTakes back the last move shown, by replaying one move fewer.
moverOfMoveWho made the record's move at move_index: the setup's player made the first, and the players took turns after it - goban has no passes, so a move's number says whose it was.
redoPlays again the next move that was undone.
continueAsPlainGoGoes on with a won Atari Go game as plain Go: the record's capture goal is dropped and the position replayed, so the win - which replaying decides from the moves - is gone, and undo and redo do not bring it back.
FloodFilledGroupA group found by flood fill: everything the forest keeps at a root, computed from the stones alone.
floodFillGroupThe group of the stone on start and its liberties, found by flood fill (the section's lesson draws it).
DisagreementKindWhat can be wrong with the forest, one kind per check of findDisagreement.
DisagreementThe first thing findDisagreement found wrong, and where.
findDisagreementCompares the forest with a flood fill of every group, and returns the first disagreement, or null when the two agree everywhere (the section's lesson lists the three passes).
PointInspectionEverything the forest says about one point: what the debug panel's inspector shows.
inspectPointInspects point (read-only: the forest is walked with parentOf, as findRoot walks it).
ForestArrowOne parent pointer of the forest: a stone, and the stone it points to.
forestArrowsEvery parent pointer of the forest - one per stone that is not a root, so a group of n stones has n - 1 - written into the caller's buffer in reading order: what the forest view draws.
pathToRootThe stones from point up to its root, parent after parent, point first and the root last, written into the caller's buffer: the walk findRoot makes, which the forest view highlights for the stone under the pointer.
diagramSymbolThe character that shows a stone in a diagram.
stoneOfSymbolThe stone a diagram's character shows, or null when the character is not one of ., X and O.
writeMoveLineWrites one move as a line of the debug panel's move log: who played where, then what it did - the groups it joined, the stones it captured, the ko point it left - each part only when there is something to say:
writeDiagramWrites the position as a diagram: the turn line, then one line per row, stones separated by spaces.
DiagramSetupWhat a diagram describes: everything a game is started from.
DiagramProblemWhy a diagram was refused, and where.
DiagramReadingWhat reading a diagram gives: the setup it describes, or why it was refused.
readDiagramReads a diagram (the section's lesson gives the format).
startGameFromDiagramStarts a game from a diagram's setup: a plain-Go record whose setup is the diagram's stones in reading order, with the diagram's player to move, and no moves yet.
ProblemKindWhich task a problem sets black (the section's lesson).
ProblemA go problem read from SGF (the section's lesson): a position, black to play, its target, which task it sets, and the points its book plays - which its region must take in, with its stones (startProblem), so that every move of the book can be played.
SgfErrorWhy an SGF text was refused as a problem.
SgfRootWhat an SGF text's root node sets up (the section's lesson): the position, black to play, its triangle marks, and where in the text the root node ends - where a property added to it goes.
readSgfRootReads an SGF text's root node (the section's lesson): its board size, its stones and its triangles, the variations after it left unread.
SgfFirstMoveA first move of an SGF problem's solution (the section's lesson): the black move that opens one of the variations after the root node, and whether a line under it ends in a comment that begins "Correct".
readSgfFirstMovesThe first moves of the variations after root's node in text, each point once - a move opening two variations is correct if either leads to "Correct".
SymmetryOne of the square board's eight symmetries (the section's lesson): four turns and four reflections.
commentSaysCorrectWhether an SGF comment says its node solves the problem: it begins "Correct", or "Also correct" for a second solution.
SolutionNodeA node of a problem's solution tree (the section's lesson): a move, whether its comment says the moves to it solve the problem, and where its first child and next sibling are in the tree's nodes.
SolutionTreeA problem's solution tree as its SGF writes it: the root's children, the first moves, and every node under them.
readSolutionTreeReads a problem's solution tree from its SGF text (the section's lesson): every node after the root with a move, each the child of the node before it in its variation, and the first nodes of a variation's variations its last node's children.
readProblemReads a go problem from an SGF text (the section's lesson): its root node (readSgfRoot), whose one mark is the target - a triangle on a white stone for a kill problem, a square on a black stone for a life problem - and the points its book's moves are played on (readSolutionTree).
readSgfCorrectLineThe moves of the first line of text's solution that reaches a comment beginning "Correct" (the section's lesson): black's and white's in turn, from the root up to and including the node with the comment.
swapColourssetup with its colours swapped, black for white and white for black, the player to move kept: a problem's twin with the other side's task - a group that had to live now to be killed, or the other way round.
ProblemSettingsHow a problem is played: the margin, in lines, its region keeps around its stones.
transformedProblemproblem drawn under symmetry: each stone, and the target, moved to its point under it.
outsideOwnerWhose the board beyond a problem's frame is (the section's lesson): the colour whose stones face the frame's open sides.
startProblemStarts a game from a problem (the section's lesson): its stones as the setup, black to play, its target, and its region - the box around its stones and its book's points, and settings.region_margin lines more - with a ring of stones just outside the region on every side the board's edge does not close, of the colour whose wall faces those sides (outsideOwner).
stonesInRegionposition's stones inside its problem's region, every point outside it empty: the stones a problem started from a played position has (puzzleAt), without the ring its first start put around the region (startProblem).
ProblemLevelHow hard a problem is, as its collection grades it: Go Game Guru's easy, intermediate and hard; other for a problem of no grade.
FamilyStartA start of a family's ladder (the section's lesson): the moves of the correct line before it, and which of its two puzzles goban_problems_tool's test of interest kept - own, black to play the problem as published from there, and twin, the move given to the other side and the colours swapped.
ProblemFamilyA published problem and the starts along its correct line that set puzzles worth asking (the section's lesson: puzzle ladders, FamilyStart).
problem_familiesgoban's published problems, by level, with their later starts, the converter's from-move files: the index's entries, their levels read from their names.
familyNamedThe published problem named name - ggg-easy-01.sgf - found at compile time: a name that is not one fails the build.
PuzzleStepOne step of a problem's ladder (the section's lesson): the position after start_moves of its correct line, black to play the problem as published - or, a twin, the position with the move given to the other side and the colours swapped, so that black plays the other task: a kill problem's twin is a life problem, and the other way round.
familyStepsThe steps of family's ladder (FamilyStart), from its first start - the full problem for a published one - to the last: the first oriented first_is_twin, each after it the other way from the one before - the kinds alternating - when that puzzle was kept, the same way when only that one was.
PuzzleA puzzle: the problem a ladder's step sets, and its book - the published tree from the step's start on, or none for a twin, whose side the book does not play.
puzzleAtThe puzzle of step in the ladder of base (the section's lesson): the base problem's position after the step's moves of correct_line, played in scratch; for a twin, the colours swapped, the target's with them, and the kind turned.
kill_problem_sgfsgoban's kill problems (problems/, whose SOURCE.md says where they come from, made by goban_problems_tool.zig): SGF texts, each with its target marked, embedded only in a program that uses them - the ones as published first, then those made from them: later starts along their correct lines, and the colour-swapped twins of life problems.
life_problem_sgfsgoban's life problems: black to play and save one black stone, the target, which a square marks.
problem_sgfsEvery one of goban's problems: the kill problems, then the life problems.
alternating_problem_sgfsgoban's problems with kill and life in turn while both last, the rest of the longer list after.
PlayoutKindHow a playout chooses its moves (the section's lesson).
MoveChoiceHow a search's move is chosen from the root's children (the section's lesson).
CutTieWho a cut-short playout goes to when both players' weakest groups have as many liberties (SearchSettings.cut_tie).
SearchSettingsWhat the search is told: how far it explores, and how its playouts are played.
playableRegionThe points a move may be played on as a rectangle: a problem's region, or the whole board.
legalMovesThe legal moves of the player to move, in the order of the points, written into out_buffer.
winningMovesThe moves that win at once for the player to move (the section's lesson): legal captures that bring their prisoners to the goal - each on the one liberty of a group of the opponent's in atari - written into out_buffer, each point once.
nearMovesThe moves a search tries first (the section's lesson): where the fight is.
CandidateKind—
CandidatesA node's candidates, and which kind of list they are.
candidateMovesA node's candidate moves, in the order its children are made (the section's lesson): the moves that win at once if there are any, in the order of the points; else every legal move, the near ones first (nearMoves) and then the rest, each in the order of the points - so that the next candidate after one can be found from it alone (nextCandidate).
nextCandidateThe first candidate of a list of kind past after - the point of the candidate before it - or null when there is none (the section's lesson): the points past it tried in order, each a legal move meeting the kind's test.
SearchNodeOne node of the search tree: a position reached from its parent's by one move, with the statistics of the playouts that went through it.
copyPositionCopies from into to the way to.* = from.* would, but of the arrays with an entry for each point only the board's points (the section's lesson): a position's arrays are as long as the largest board's, and an 8 x 8 board uses 64 of their 361 entries.
positionFingerprintA 64-bit hash of what decides a position's future: the board's size and stones, the player to move, the ko point, the prisoners, the capture goal, a problem's target and region, and the winner.
SearchTreeHeaderWhat a tree is besides its nodes: how many are in use, which game it belongs to, and its generator's state.
SearchTreeThe search's tree: its header, and its nodes, the first header.node_count of nodes in use.
explorationFactorThe factor of UCB1's exploration term that every child of one parent shares (the section's lesson): c * sqrt(ln(parent's visits)).
upperConfidenceBoundUCB1 (the section's lesson) for a child visited at least once: its win rate plus exploration_factor - the parent's explorationFactor - over the square root of its visits.
isOwnEyeA legal move of the player to move, drawn uniformly at random, or null when none is legal.
ladderCapturesWhether the group on defender_point is captured in a ladder (the section's lesson; after KataGo's searchIsLadderCaptured): a depth-first search of the fight over a group of one or two liberties, each side's moves only the ones that matter.
urgentMoveThe heavy playout's urgent move (the section's lesson), or null when there is none: the capture of a group of the opponent's in atari, else the escape of a group of the player to move's in atari - each a legal move on the group's one liberty, drawn at random when there are several of a kind.
fightingMoveThe next move of a playout of kind, or null when no move is legal.
atariFightingMoveThe move that keeps fighting in a game of Atari Go (the section's lesson), played where the search's own choice would fight nothing (playedMove): it attacks the opponent's weakest group, or defends its own.
playOutA playout (the section's lesson): moves chosen as settings.playout_kind says, played on position until a player reaches the goal, no move is legal, or settings.playout_move_cap moves have been played.
runIterationOne iteration of the search (the section's lesson): selection, expansion, a playout and backpropagation.
runIterationsiteration_count iterations of the search from root_position (see runIteration).
mostVisitedChildThe most visited child of parent_index - the newest of equals, as with childWithHighestBound - or null when it has no child.
rootMoveIsForcedWhether the root's move is forced: one candidate - a capture that wins, or the only legal move - leaves the search nothing to choose, so it plays the move at once instead of spending the move's time on it.
mostVisitedMoveThe root's most visited child's move (mostVisitedChild): the move a search makes by the visits alone, which go to the move whose wins hold up.
rootChildThe root's child for move, or null when the root has none.
rootChildWinRateHow often the player who makes move won, in the root's child for it (SearchNode.winRate); zero when the root has no such child.
chosenMoveThe move a search chooses (MoveChoice): the most visited child's, or the one whose win rate less settings.confidence_stdevs standard errors is highest, among the children with at least settings.confidence_min_share of the most visited child's visits.
playedMoveThe move the computer plays from a search of position (the section's lesson): the chosen move (chosenMove), except in the two cases where the fighting move cannot change the result (SearchSettings.lost_below_rate): a lost position, every move losing - the fighting move among them all; and a tenuki between equals - the fighting move among the moves near the fight as good as it (equalMovesNearFight).
searchIsSettledWhether the search has nothing left to find: the root's move is forced.
takeUpSubtreeThe subtree of old_tree reached from its root by moves_since_root, copied into node_buffer as a tree of its own (the section's lesson): the node reached is its root, node 0, and everything under it follows, with its visits and half-wins, its children in their order, and the old tree's generator state.
continueSearchThe search for the game in record continued (the section's lesson): last_tree - the tree the last search returned, if any - taken up where it fits, a fresh tree seeded with seed where it does not, and iteration_count iterations run on it.
writeTreeWrites tree as bytes (the section's lesson): its header, then its nodes in use.
readTreeHeaderThe header of the tree writeTree wrote into bytes, or null when the bytes are too short to hold one.
readTreeThe tree writeTree wrote into bytes, its nodes copied into node_buffer; null when the bytes are not a tree's - too short, or not as long as their node count says - or the tree has more nodes than node_buffer.
viewTreeThe tree writeTree wrote into bytes, its nodes read where they are, with no copy: the bytes are aligned for nodes, and the header's size is a whole number of node alignments.
SearchJobHeaderWhat a search job is told besides its payload (the section's lesson): an extern struct, as a job's header must be.
writeSearchPayloadWrites a search job's payload (the section's lesson): record - its board, goal, setup stones and the moves played, field by field - then last_tree_bytes, the last tree as writeTree wrote it (empty for none).
readSearchPayloadReads a search job's payload into record - its moves recorded up to the moves played, none beyond - and returns the bytes after it, the last tree's (empty when there is none); null when the payload is not one: too short for what it says it holds, or with a board size, a turn or a count no record can have.
searchJobThe search job (the section's lesson; listed in kernels.zig): the record and the last tree read from payload, continueSearch run with header's settings, count and seed, and the tree it returns written to out.
search_job_kernelsThe search's job kernels, by name (the section's lesson): the wasm export of each is zimr_job_<name>.
search_job_registryThe registry the search's jobs are submitted through.
BoardStyleHow the board looks.
BoardLayoutWhere the board is on the screen this frame.
CaptureColumnsWhere the captured stones are drawn this frame.
fittedSlotSpacingThe slot spacing a column of slot_count stones wants (the lesson above): one captured stone apart while they fit in the board's height, closer when they do not, but never closer than slot_spacing_min_radii.
starPointsThe star points of a board of this size, written into the caller's buffer (the section's lesson draws them).
TexturesThe pictures the board is drawn with.
OverlayOptionsWhich overlays are drawn: the player's choice, in the pause window.
HoverClockHow long the pointer has been on the point it is on: the clock of the hover overlays' fade and pulse.
libertyOverlayGroupsThe groups whose liberties are shown (the overlays' lesson), as their roots in shown_position, written into the caller's buffer: the groups touching the ghost's point, of both players, and the group of a stone under the pointer - each once.
LibertyLabelA liberty count shown on the board (the overlays' lesson): the stone it is written on - the group's stone beside the ghost, or the stone under the pointer - and the group's liberties before the ghost's move and after it.
libertyLabels—
GhostThe ghost: the stone the player to move would place, drawn where the pointer would place it.
updateGhostMoves the ghost toward target_point, the point under the pointer when a move is legal there, or hides it when there is none.
FlightPlacementWhere a flying stone is drawn: its centre and its width.
FlightOne captured stone on its way to its column.
FlightsThe stones in flight.
landedPrisonersHow many of capturer's prisoners have landed in its column: those captured, less those still in flight.
shownSlotsHow many slots a side's column shows: its landed prisoners, or the Atari Go goal if that is more.
pointerIsPresentIs there a pointer over the screen at all this frame? A mouse always is; a finger only while it touches - the frame of its lifting included, so that a release is read where it happened.
aimedPositionThe position on the screen a pointer aims at (the section's lesson): the pointer itself for a mouse; for a finger, finger_lift_px above it, the lift shrinking to the distance left to the window's bottom edge.
BoardPointerWhat the pointer did over the board this frame.
isPlayableMay the player to move play on point now: is the move legal, and has the record room for it?
dropPointThe point a stone would drop on for a pointer at aimed_px (the section's lesson).
steadyDropPointThe drop point, held steady (the section's lesson): previous, the ghost's point last frame, is kept while it is still playable and within reach and no other point is nearer the pointer by hysteresis_spacings; otherwise the drop point now (dropPoint).
ShownPointA point the ghost showed, and when: how long after the press.
StoneHoldA stone picked up by a press, and where the ghost has shown it since: the last frames' points, each with how long after the press it was shown, so that a release can play where the ghost was a moment before (the section's lesson).
FingerGestureWhether a second finger has cancelled the gesture under way (the section's lesson): set the frame two fingers are down, cleared once none is.
applyPointerOne frame of the pointer on the board (the section's lesson): a press over a playable point picks the stone up; while it is held, the ghost's points are remembered; a release plays it on the point the ghost showed release_delay_seconds before, if that point is playable.
ControlsStyleThe sizes of the buttons and the pause window, in units of the interface's font size, and how the font size follows the window.
GameKindThe main menu's choices, kept from one game to the next: the board's size, and whether the game is Atari Go and to how many captures.
PlayersWho plays the game being played: its kind, and the person's colour when the computer plays the other.
nextTakeWhich of take_count takes to play next, from a random roll: any of them the first time, and after that any but the previous one - each of the others equally likely.
PaywallA window go puzzles hero opens once a session's first games are played (AppVariant.paywall): a message, a link opened in a new tab, and a password box.
RewardSettingsgo puzzles hero's reward for a new level (AppVariant.reward_images): when a win fills the row of progress's last place, the game waits delay_seconds - the full row seen - then shows one of the pictures, drawn at random, with the reward's sound: it comes down from above the window to its centre in enter_seconds, stays hold_seconds, and goes on down and out in exit_seconds, a backdrop darkening and clearing with it.
RewardPhaseWhere a reward stands, by the seconds since the row's last place was filled (rewardPhase).
rewardPhaseThe phase of a reward elapsed_seconds after the row's last place was filled (RewardSettings).
rewardOffsetWhere the reward's picture is show_seconds after it began to show (RewardSettings), as a share of the window's height below the centre: from -1, just above the window, to 0 while it comes in - fast at first, slowing into place (ease out, the cube of what is left) - 0 while it stays, and from 0 to 1, out at the bottom, while it goes - slow at first, gathering speed (ease in, the cube of the share gone).
rewardBackdropAlphaHow dark the reward's backdrop is show_seconds after the picture began to show (RewardSettings): darkening from clear to black as it comes in, black while it stays, clearing as it goes.
OutpostLadderA ladder of games that follows the results (AppVariant.outpost_ladder; the section's lesson): each rung a split of scattered stones between the person and the computer, and each rung played twice - the person moving first, then moving second.
HeroRoundThe rounds of go puzzles hero (the section's lesson), each one's successor drawn at random (RoundChances).
RoundChancesThe chances of go puzzles hero's next round after each kind (the section's lesson): a row for each kind - its chances of a fight, a life puzzle and a kill puzzle next, in the order of HeroRound, adding up to 1.
nextRoundThe round after round (RoundChances): draw, a number from 0 up to 1, falls in one kind's share of the row - the shares laid end to end in the order of HeroRound.
RatingSettingsHow go puzzles hero's rating moves, and what a round draws from (the section's lesson).
HintSettingsThe hint in go puzzles hero's puzzles (the section's lesson): a button offered once the round's problem has been failed after_failures times - on any of its ladder's steps - which shows the move to play as a blue dot for show_seconds: the book's next correct move while the moves so far follow the book, else the search's choice for the player from a search of search_iterations (heroHintMove).
HeroFlowSettingsHow go puzzles hero moves on from a puzzle it cannot get past (the section's lesson): a step failed max_step_failures times in a row - the bottom step of a ladder, repeated after each failure, most often - ends the round, and the next is drawn, so that no puzzle holds the player for good.
HeroLevelgo puzzles hero's level and its progress (the section's lesson): every places wins - a round's full problem solved at its first try, or a fight won - raise the level by one, from 1; the wins since the level began fill the row of progress, which empties as the level rises.
heroLevelThe level and progress wins make (HeroLevel), with places wins a level.
ProgressRowWhere go puzzles hero's row of progress is drawn (heroProgressRow): the first place's centre, the step to the next, a stone's size, and how many places.
heroProgressRowgo puzzles hero's row of progress (the section's lesson): controls.progress_places places in a line along the bottom, at the buttons' height, centred between the hint's place on the left and Next on the right - their sizes as submitHeroButtons makes them - each place at most controls.progress_spacing_fonts from the next and no wider than the room between the buttons allows, a stone filling nine tenths of its place.
HeroOutcomeHow a puzzle of go puzzles hero's ended, for its ladder.
nextHeroStepThe step a ladder of step_count steps goes to after its step step_index ended outcome (the section's lesson): one earlier after a solution, towards the full problem - or null, the round over, when that was the full problem; one later after giving up, the last at the most.
HeroStepMoveWhere go puzzles hero goes after a step (heroStepAfter): the ladder's next step, or null for the next round, and the failures in a row the step then has.
heroStepAfterThe move after step step_index of step_count ended with outcome (the section's lesson, nextHeroStep), the step failed step_failures times in a row before: a failure more, and at flow.max_step_failures the round moves on; otherwise the ladder's next step, its count of failures kept only if it is the same step again - the bottom step, after a failure - and reset for another.
RatingWindowThe families a round may draw from: first up to end, in the order of difficulty.
ratingWindowThe window of family_count families, in order of difficulty, that a round at rating draws from (RatingSettings): window_share of them, centred on the rating's place, and shifted, not cut, at either end - so that a window always holds as many.
ratedPlaceThe rating after a round's full problem ended at its first try: up a step when it was solved, down when not - a step for each of the failures_in_a_row, this one included, up to settings.max_streak_steps - within 0 and 1 (RatingSettings).
HeroProgressWhat go puzzles hero keeps between runs (the section's lesson): the count of wins - the one number it shows - the puzzle rating, the outpost ladder's position and whether the paywall's password has been typed, saved together as one value (z.saveValue) of nine bytes: a version, the ladder's position, the rating in ten-thousandths, the count, little-endian, then a byte of flags - its lowest bit the password's.
FixedGameA game an app plays from its start, with no main menu (AppVariant.fixed_game): its settings, and who plays it.
ProblemPlaySettingsHow a problem's game judges its player's moves after the problem is solved.
InterfaceWhat interface one of goban's apps has.
AppVariantWhat makes one of goban's apps (the section's lesson): goban is the default, and another example makes its own from the same code with appFor - the rules, the search, the board, the input and the sounds all goban's.
centredWindowTopWhere a centred window's top goes, for a window window_height_px tall in a screen screen_height_px tall, kept margin_px from the screen's top and bottom: the room the window leaves between the margins goes a third above it and two thirds below, so it sits high, where a menu is looked for.
ProblemEvidenceWhat a problem's game has shown so far, for isProblemSolved to judge.
isProblemSolvedWhether a problem is solved (the section's lesson).
panicThe panic handler: the examples' own, which reports through the engine, when this file is the program; std's default when this file is built as its own test, which has no engine to report through.
appForOne of goban's apps, as variant makes it.
appThe program, as the runner (a page, a window, a headless run) or the launcher starts it: goban, with its interface.
